[2026-05-03T21:50|whoami|62370] I’m 99% sure that “half” and “better half” refer to two private keys. The half is the original vanity address 1GSMG1…, while the better half is 17ucy…. \n  \n Most likely, the creator first generated a vanity private key for 1GSMG. After finding that key, he then used its secp256k1 modular complement (n - k) mod n to derive the second one. \n  \n That would mean both wallets are mathematically linked and actually come from the same cryptographic source, just expressed as two opposite private keys. This fits the yin-yang hint perfectly: two complementary halves of one whole.

[2026-05-04T12:43|Sparky|62406] padding always exists, it is 16 bytes of value 16 when data length is 16, 32, 48, 64, etc. So a 32 bytes key still needs 48 bytes ciphertext.  Our blobs hold between 64 and 79 decrypted bytes. You will get 79 bytes of garbage with valid padding 255 times out of 65536 passwords. Or 78 bytes garbage 255 times out of 16777216 passwords. Anyway, it's infinitely easier to break ECDLP then to break the AES blobs.

Lack of transparent legal identity \n    The platform does not appear to publicly disclose a verifiable legal entity responsible for operating the service. There is no clearly identifiable company registration number, corporate structure, responsible management, or transparent jurisdiction of operation visible on the website. This creates significant uncertainty regarding legal accountability. \n  \n 2. Hidden ownership and anonymity \n    Public WHOIS/domain registration data indicates that the ownership information of the gsmg.io domain is protected through privacy masking services rather than openly identifying the operator. While privacy protection itself is not illegal, in combination with cryptocurrency-related prize pools and anonymous administration it substantially increases operational and reputational risk. \n  \n 3. Absence of independent verification \n    There does not appear to be any reliable independent audit confirming: \n  \n - the legitimacy of the reward pool, \n - custody and ownership of the BTC involved, \n - solvency of the organizers, \n - or the existence of enforceable payout guarantees. \n  \n Participants are effectively expected to trust anonymous or pseudonymous operators without institutional safeguards. \n  \n 4. Potential legal and financial exposure \n    Participation in such activities may be highly risky because the origin of the cryptocurrency used for rewards cannot be independently verified. It cannot be excluded that some funds could originate from: \n  \n - theft, \n - fraud, \n - ransomware activity, \n - unauthorized wallet access, \n - money laundering schemes, \n - or other illicit blockchain activity. \n  \n In many jurisdictions, interacting with illicit crypto assets — even indirectly and unknowingly — may create legal complications, compliance exposure, frozen exchange accounts, or investigative scrutiny. \n  \n 5. Reputational concerns and scam indicators \n    Several characteristics commonly associated with high-risk or potentially fraudulent crypto operations appear present: \n  \n - anonymous administration, \n - unclear jurisdiction, \n - lack of corporate transparency, \n - unverifiable reward structure, \n - absence of regulated oversight, \n - and reliance on speculative community trust. \n  \n Some online reputation analysis services classify the domain or operation as questionable or high-risk and recommend caution. \n  \n 6. Technical plausibility concerns \n    The challenge itself raises an additional logical issue. If the advertised reward is genuine and technically accessible, then the solution should theoretically be obtainable through legitimate cryptographic or computational means. However, if the challenge is intentionally structured in a way that is practically impossible while still attracting public attention, then concerns regarding deceptive marketing or engagement manipulation become justified. \n  \n For these reasons, extreme caution is recommended before: \n  \n - sending any funds, \n - connecting wallets, \n - sharing sensitive information, \n - running untrusted binaries, \n - or investing substantial time or computing resources into the challenge. \n  \n At minimum, any legitimate operation involving significant cryptocurrency rewards should provide: \n  \n - transparent legal identity, \n - verifiable ownership, \n - independently auditable prize custody, \n - clear legal terms, \n - jurisdictional disclosures, \n - and publicly accountable operators.

[2026-05-22T00:06|Artem|63463] In ECC BTC curve, for each point on the curve given by x, there are two values of y: \n  \n positive Y - the upper point \n  \n negative Y - the lower point \n  \n In a physical or philosophical sense, this can be seen as a duality, two opposite “halves” of a single entity. \n  \n Similarly: \n  \n positive Y - Yin \n  \n negative Y - Yang \n  \n Since they together form the complete point on the curve, this resembles the Yin-Yang concept: \n  \n Two opposites complementing each other. \n  \n On the curve, the x-coordinate defines the entire duality of Y.... \n  \n Theory of Everything \n  \n In physics  it is a hypothetical unified model that combines all fundamental interactions. \n  \n In cryptography/puzzle context  it may mean the need to find a single unifying concept that brings together the clues: philosophy, mathematics, cryptography. \n  \n Yin and Yang, duality \n  \n In ECC (elliptic curve cryptography), for each point with coordinate x, there exist two values of y (one positive, one negative). \n  \n This literally reflects the idea of two opposites that are part of a single whole (like yin and yang). \n  \n In philosophy: harmony arises through the balance of two poles. \n  \n In cryptography: a point and its reflection have the same x, but different y. \n  \n The “c” point on the curve (key generation) \n  \n All keys in Bitcoin are born from a single base point (G) on the secp256k1 curve. \n  \n This resembles the analogy with the Big Bang: from one point (a singularity) emerges the entire Universe (all possible keys/addresses). \n  \n It can be interpreted as “an infinite multitude born from one source.” \n  \n Opposites as “attraction” \n  \n On the curve, a point and its reflection across the X-axis are always connected. \n  \n In philosophy - opposites interact and are inseparable. \n  \n In cryptography - without knowing the correct interpretation (which y to use), one cannot properly reconstruct the private key. \n  \n  \n Public key for  1GSMG1JC9wtdSwfwApgj2xcmJPAwx7prBe its \n  \n 04f4d1bbd91e65e2a019566a17574e97dae908b784b388891848007e4f55d5a4649c73d25fc5ed8fd7227cab0be4e576c0c6404db5aa546286563e4be12bf33559 \n  \n Now we can decompose the curve into points \n  \n X   =      f4d1bbd91e65e2a019566a17574e97dae908b784b388891848007e4f55d5a464 \n  \n Y   =      9c73d25fc5ed8fd7227cab0be4e576c0c6404db5aa546286563e4be12bf33559 \n  \n Y-  =      638c2da03a127028dd8354f41b1a893f39bfb24a55ab9d79a9c1b41dd40cc6d6 \n  \n  \n It is necessary to conduct an analysis of all addresses for a specific x \n  \n grass - [2026-05-22T01:57|Artem|63476] You are mixing two different layers of the argument. \n First: yes — in strict ECC terms you are correct. \n The relationship between keys must be proven through group arithmetic, not narrative interpretations. And there is no evidence that 1GSMG and 17ucy are directly related via scalar transformations, bit inversion, or simple affine operations. I agree with that part. \n Second: my interpretation was not claiming a proven cryptographic relation between private keys. It was a structural hypothesis about how the puzzle might encode information, not a claim that ECC arithmetic directly maps 1:1 to narrative symbolism. \n What I am exploring is whether the representation layer of elliptic curve points (not the scalar structure) can be used as an encoding space - including: \n point doubling / halving paths on the curve \n traversal of the group graph via repeated addition/subtraction \n and the dual representation of a point through its y± symmetry \n In that sense, my method is: \n use ECC operations (P, 2P, 3P, … and inverse operations where defined) to explore the curve structure as a graph \n and analyze both valid y values (y and -y mod p) as two complementary representations of the same x-coordinate \n  \n So yes: \n no direct cryptographic relation between the keys is proven - agreed \n bit inversion and simple transformations do not match - agreed \n But my focus is not on deriving one private key from another. \n It is on whether the puzzle encodes signals in how curve points are traversed and how the dual y-representation is interpreted within that traversal space.

[2026-06-04T00:43|Sparky|64576] Only if you already know that you are decrypting with the correct key, the padding gets irrelevant.
[2026-06-04T00:44|Sparky|64577] You need to decrypt the padding block to check the padding
[2026-06-04T00:44|Sparky|64578] Otherwise, AES doesn't have any built-in verification mechanism. Any key will decrypt anything to whatever.
[2026-06-04T00:45|Sparky|64579] this is how on the fly encryption works, like disk encryption.
[2026-06-04T00:50|Sparky|64580] and as a general shortcut to attack AES with PKCS#7 padding: simply decrypt the padding block. If padding fails, the key is wrong. No need to go through the entire long message, last two blocks are enough for this.

[2026-06-08T05:27|Denis Golovkin|64725] For output of the cosmic duality to be a *.bin file, it should include some file system. I doubt, that one can encode any filesystem in that volume of data. \n It would be much more accurate to say, that the output of cosmic duality is ususally a binary garbage, because AES is't designed to ensure correct decryption result, it only provides some probabilistic checks.
[2026-06-08T05:33|Denis Golovkin|64726] Also, a *.bin file could be an output of an LLM, that tried hard to decrypt the blob, got some outputs that doesn't lead to error code returned from openssl and decided to wrap it's work into a *.bin file, to make you happy. \n LLM's are trained to use their last chance and provide any solution that looks truthful, because that behavior can lead to correct answer in choose A/B/C/D tests or even can lead another LLM to give higher score when evaluating results.

[2026-06-09T09:08|gnosis|64922] We’re still chipping away at this offline. We’re pretty sure the “story” really is stored in dbbib → faed → btcseed/post, not in some other random string. From there we get stable S / H / L rails with the usual markers (SENDTHE / TOSETHEX / BLUENET), and a few check formulas that hit cleanly (same tail windows, keep gates, that sort of thing) — but they read more like verifiers than the final AES key. Puzzle IR @54–56 and spiral vs. row-major on faed change real bytes in that zone; trifid still throws CHAO / HALF-shaped windows if you use the obvious keys. We also see VIC + reins line up with the “private key / half” wording — again, semantic, not BLOB yet. No BLOB1 for us — so we’re probably missing one last binary compose step Jrk meant by yinyang. If anyone’s sitting on a clean dbbib/faed read or a post-BTCSEED transform that isn’t just another sha16 sweep, happy to compare notes. 🤝

[2026-06-20T11:27|Denis Golovkin|65604] Sure it's easily bisected using a newton method + some Metropolis-Gastings sampling and charge-flipping algorithm (here you'll need to perform few jumps between time and frequency domains, per one sampling step). And this allows you to know one bit of a private key at a time in a few seconds on a modern PC. Then just apply divide and conquier strategy to recrsively get all the bits of private key, given that you know public key. Good luck.

[2026-07-02T09:45|حسین ▪️|66244] # Part4 Decoding Method \n  \n This document explains how Part4 was decoded and how the encoding pattern was identified. \n  \n --- \n  \n ## 1. First layer: Bifid Decode \n  \n Part4 is first decoded with Bifid Cipher Decode. The output begins with: \n  \n btcseed... \n  \n This is the main clue: the final payload is expected to be a Bitcoin seed, most likely a BIP39 mnemonic. \n  \n --- \n  \n ## 2. Splitting the interleaved channels \n  \n After the Bifid step, the Part4 output starts like this: \n  \n btcseeddeoemckeadhbschdkbdcsdkdvbxcpcochcrdicibqee... \n  \n For the hidden channel alignment, remove the first six characters: \n  \n btcsee \n  \n Then split the remaining text into alternating characters: \n  \n rest = decoded_part4[6:] \n channel_b = rest[::2] \n channel_c = rest[1::2] \n  \n The channel_b stream contains only four symbols: \n  \n b c d e \n  \n So this is a 4-symbol alphabet, meaning each symbol can encode exactly 2 bits. \n  \n --- \n  \n ## 3. Four-symbol to binary mapping \n  \n The correct mapping is: \n  \n d = 00 \n c = 01 \n b = 10 \n e = 11 \n  \n Or numerically: \n  \n d, c, b, e  ->  0, 1, 2, 3 \n  \n --- \n  \n ## 4. Locating the valid BIP39 payload \n  \n Inside the 4-symbol stream, the valid payload starts at: \n  \n offset = 27 \n  \n Take the next 132 symbols: \n  \n cdcdccecdcebcdcccedecededdcbcbbebbeeedbcbbcebeebbedddeccecccccbdcccbeeceeccbbeeededbcdbdecbebbedcbcebebbcecccededddcceebecccdbdddecc \n  \n Since each symbol is 2 bits: \n  \n 132 symbols × 2 bits = 264 bits \n  \n And 264 bits is exactly the length of a 24-word BIP39 mnemonic: \n  \n 24 words × 11 bits = 264 bits \n  \n --- \n  \n ## 5. Converting to bits \n  \n Using d=00, c=01, b=10, e=11, we get: \n  \n 010001000101110100011110010001010111001101110011000001100110101110101111110010011010011110111110101100000011010111010101010110000101011011110111110101101011111100110010010010001101101110101100011001111011101001110101011100110000000101111110110101010010000000110101 \n  \n --- \n  \n ## 6. Splitting into 11-bit BIP39 indexes \n  \n Each 11-bit group is an index into the standard BIP39 English wordlist: \n  \n | # | 11-bit | index | BIP39 word | \n |---:|---|---:|---| \n | 1 | 01000100010 | 546 | dust | \n | 2 | 11101000111 | 1863 | trophy | \n | 3 | 10010001010 | 1162 | mule | \n | 4 | 11100110111 | 1847 | tragic | \n | 5 | 00110000011 | 387 | corn | \n | 6 | 00110101110 | 430 | cupboard | \n | 7 | 10111111001 | 1529 | sand | \n | 8 | 00110100111 | 423 | crunch | \n | 9 | 10111110101 | 1525 | salt | \n | 10 | 10000001101 | 1037 | like | \n | 11 | 01110101010 | 938 | inspire | \n | 12 | 10110000101 | 1413 | radar | \n | 13 | 01101111011 | 891 | hunt | \n | 14 | 11101011010 | 1882 | twice | \n | 15 | 11111100110 | 2022 | wolf | \n | 16 | 01001001000 | 584 | empower | \n | 17 | 11011011101 | 1757 | sweet | \n | 18 | 01100011001 | 793 | glimpse | \n | 19 | 11101110100 | 1908 | update | \n | 20 | 11101010111 | 1879 | turtle | \n | 21 | 00110000000 | 384 | copy | \n | 22 | 10111111011 | 1531 | satoshi | \n | 23 | 01010100100 | 676 | fee | \n | 24 | 00000110101 | 53 | allow | \n  \n --- \n  \n ## 7. Final Part4 mnemonic \n  \n dust trophy mule tragic corn cupboard sand crunch salt like inspire radar hunt twice wolf empower sweet glimpse update turtle copy satoshi fee allow \n  \n The consecutive words below strongly confirm the solution, especially together with the initial btcseed clue: \n  \n satoshi fee \n  \n --- \n  \n ## 8. Checksum validation \n  \n A 24-word BIP39 mnemonic contains 264 bits: \n  \n 256 bits entropy + 8 bits checksum \n  \n Recovered entropy: \n  \n 445d1e457373066bafc9a7beb035d55856f7d6bf3248dbac67ba7573017ed520 \n  \n Checksum bits from the mnemonic: \n  \n 00110101 \n  \n The checksum computed from SHA256(entropy) is also: \n  \n 00110101 \n  \n So the mnemonic is valid. \n  \n --- \n  \n ## 9. Minimal reproduction code
[2026-07-02T09:45|حسین ▪️|66245] import hashlib \n  \n words = open("bip39.txt").read().splitlines()  # standard BIP39 English wordlist \n  \n channel = "cdcdccecdcebcdcccedecededdcbcbbebbeeedbcbbcebeebbedddeccecccccbdcccbeeceeccbbeeededbcdbdecbebbedcbcebebbcecccededddcceebecccdbdddecc" \n  \n mapping = { \n     "d": "00", \n     "c": "01", \n     "b": "10", \n     "e": "11", \n } \n  \n bits = "".join(mapping[ch] for ch in channel) \n indexes = [int(bits[i:i+11], 2) for i in range(0, len(bits), 11)] \n mnemonic = [words[i] for i in indexes] \n  \n print(" ".join(mnemonic)) \n  \n entropy_bits = bits[:256] \n checksum_bits = bits[256:] \n entropy = int(entropy_bits, 2).to_bytes(32, "big") \n expected_checksum = bin(int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(entropy).digest(), "big"))[2:].zfill(256)[:8] \n  \n print(entropy.hex()) \n print(checksum_bits, expected_checksum)

[2026-07-02T11:24|Sebastian|66264] I reproduced every step against the canonical bifid output. The channel split is genuinely real — after the interleave split, one channel really is restricted to {b,c,d,e}. That's a known structural property (it's the 2-bit selector stream of the value/selector pairs). But the mnemonic conclusion unfortunately doesn't hold: \n  \n - The "valid checksum" is not a confirmation. I swept ALL 24 possible symbol→2-bit mappings × all 151 possible offsets in that channel (3,624 windows total): 13 of them produce checksum-valid BIP39 mnemonics purely by chance (expected value: 3624/256 ≈ 14). The dcbe/offset-27 find is statistically exactly one of these chance windows. \n - Offset 27 and length 132 are free parameters with no derivation, and the window even straddles the block90/tail structure boundary and ignores the Z separator. \n - Most importantly: no prize address hit. BIP44/49/84 (external+internal, first 5 indices) → nothing; the entropy as a raw private key → nothing. And the main prize address is a vanity address, so it cannot come from a BIP39 seed at all — only the secondary 17ucy… address could, and it doesn't match. \n - "satoshi fee" appearing as consecutive words isn't surprising when drawing 24 words from a wordlist full of bitcoin-themed words. \n  \n So: real structure, but the seed is a classic BIP39-checksum overfit. Happy to share the verification scripts if anyone wants to double-check.

[2026-07-13T20:43|Vasilis Dragon|66722] Here you go. step by step. two inputs, both exactly 91 chars, lowercase letters only: \n dbbi -> sha256 71fe46259e270c113529dfaded4b59c59a9dffd826a7202ab07fc498b6a2c5ca \n phase 3.2 plaintext (the "in case you manage to crack this ... they also need funds to live" line, call it M91) -> sha256 2fbfbef7442558f17ecc866a0b89ed491c41abc042df4d41452835adff09e9d3 \n  \n step 1. A = dbbi minus M91, letter by letter, a=0..z=25, mod 26. \n A = VOZIJBDTIQBRGVEOMZNBC YOUWON XCPKW... \n sha256(A) = f17857308a886531637b33c9ddb92952206c286519412278ba9f836ffc69336e \n YOUWON sits at index 21 (zero based). split is 21 / 49 / 21 = 91, and 21 = C(7,2). middle 49 drop into the 7x7 frame, rows MMJIOLS cols JPJSROH. \n  \n step 2. two different rails, both point at index 21. \n borrow rail = the underflow bit from that same subtraction (1 when dbbi_i < M91_i). only run of 7+ ones in the whole 91 bits starts at 21 -> 1111111 = 127 = DEL. sha256 ccf4c906024ce25a4cad66f2a78deecc216a39a791d5c1402f30374ee749ac0e \n vic rail = phase 3.2 checkerboard codeword width, top row FUBCDORA = 0, everything else = 1. its longest run, 9 ones, also starts at 21. sha256 2d3b44885d39ccc2471ef0e67bab18ce8f087d816ea4cbb07cf21be77d5baee3 \n theyre not fully independent (both built off M91) but two separate definitions landing on the same index is the part that got me \n  \n step 3. faed self keyed bifid -> BTCSEED||P1||z. first 98 chars = 49 digraphs, the single z at position 97 forces where the window ends. rail2 = second char of each digraph = tsedomkahshkdskvxpohr... take the first row, reverse it: tsedomk -> KMODEST. sha256 7782502c6a6ad8b6b33f3d517b0ee217a15fef516e4e4d0f2f921ffa66c33974 \n  \n step 4, and being straight this is the soft one. bifid square from dbbi[:13] = dbifh cegak lmnop qrstu vwxyz. K is at (2,5). the DEL from step 2 knocks K out of KMODEST leaving MODEST, and you read the removed K coords (2,5) as B,E under a1z26 = BE. gives BE MODEST. im reading those coords because i already know what the answer should be, so call it a convention not a forced step. everything up to KMODEST stands on its own, step 4 is the reach. \n  \n end result YOU WON - BE MODEST, sha256(YOUWONBEMODEST) = e8f325105e899ed8505fc60dcf2d8c24cda35e0ff4a998688b9924b4d1161ecd \n comes straight out of dbbi + the phase 3.2 text

[2026-07-19T20:49|None|67113] How It Was Derived \n The creator’s Telegram message says: \n “Hint: another door might be found on {1},{4},{21}” \n  \n We applied those positions to the unresolved DBBI stream, using normal human indexing: position 1 means the first character, not position 0. \n  \n DBBI: \n dbbibfbhccbegbihabebeihbeggegebebbgehhebhhfbabfdhbeffcdbbfcccgbfbeeggecbedcibfbffgigbeeeabe \n  \n The relevant characters are: \n Position \n Character \n 1 d \n 4 i \n 21 e \n  \n Therefore: \n DBBI[1] + DBBI[4] + DBBI[21] = d + i + e = DIE \n This is also why the result is persuasive: using zero-based indexing gives BBI, while applying the same positions to FAED gives FDA, neither of which forms a meaningful word. \n  \n Important Qualification \n DIE is a mechanically reproducible extraction, but the creator never explicitly said “apply {1},{4},{21} to DBBI.” So the careful classification is: \n Creator-authenticated: the {1},{4},{21} hint. \n Mechanically derived: DBBI[1,4,21] = DIE. \n Inferred: DBBI is the intended receiver. \n Unproven: what DIE instructs us to do next. \n  \n We tested interpreting DIE as deletion, zeroing, selection, and a Bifid/sumlist control. Those produced no key or exact AES result. \n  \n Is this BS or anything useful?
